US8664 TUO CHENGTEAM 1 (USA)111700Z AUG 26

WebNX, Inc. (webnx.com) — Sales Intelligence Dossier

Prepared for: Supermicro Sales Team 1 (USA) · Officer US8664 Tuo Cheng · Date: 2026-08-04 Method: Researched through corporate history, financial and registry records, U.S. political leanings, legislative and policy positions, and the company's relationships with its customers. This is a privately held company with no SEC filings — evidence comes from state business registries, ARIN/PeeringDB, the provider's own published server specifications and pricing, job postings, community forums, and FEC records. Every fact carries its source + date inline. GAP = not found in verified material. No fabrication. Territory: Ogden, Utah — West Coast South excl. CA = T1 | T31. Team 1 can register directly. CRM status: Verified 2026-08-03 in salesleads Search (Type = All): no lead, no account, no do-not-call record — never registered.

1. Bottom line

WebNX, Inc. is a privately held, founder-led dedicated-server, colocation and GPU/AI hosting provider headquartered inside its own flagship datacenter at 119 North 600 West, Bldg 3B, Ogden, Utah, with leased footprints in Los Angeles (Equinix LA3), New York, Salt Lake City and Dallas, running AS18450 and self-describing "thousands of high-end servers globally" on a staff of 11–50 (LinkedIn company page, self-reported; BBB profile, file opened 2020-06-03). Commercially this is a platform-conversion target, not an incumbent-defence account — WebNX is a confirmed component-level whitebox integrator that has never named a system vendor anywhere in public, sits inside Team 1's own territory, and has never once appeared in the CRM. The single strongest piece of evidence is WebNX's own publicly priced flagship AI SKU — dual AMD EPYC 9575F (128C/256T), 1.5 TB DDR5, 2 × 15 TB Gen5 NVMe, 4 × NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 96 GB at $7,999/month (Ogden GPU & AI store, read 2026-08-03) — which proves they already buy and resell current-generation Turin + Blackwell platforms, while the same catalogue still carries 2017-silicon V100s and consumer RTX 3090s with zero H100 / H200 / L40S coverage. The one thing that could kill the deal is the channel: a fleet this size with no CRM record at all points at purchase through distribution, so under Rule 8 the distributor position must be cleared before the first call, or the engagement becomes a channel conflict rather than a sale.

3. Corrections to the working list

Only the rows that concern WebNX. Verdict scale: confirmed = first-hand named disclosure or multiple independent corroboration · partly confirmed = core is true but the framing needs fixing · contradicted = evidence runs against the working list · unverified = cannot be established from public sources and must not be stated to the customer.

# Working-list claim Verdict Evidence and the correct wording
1 WebNX uses Supermicro and/or Dell hardware UNVERIFIED — do not state this to the customer Across 16+ searches and direct fetches of WebNX's Ogden custom, GPU/AI, storage, Intel and colocation store pages, its Utah datacenter page, its LinkedIn page and both live Indeed postings, WebNX never names a system vendor, chassis model or motherboard brand. Its WHMCS knowledgebase is empty ("No Articles Found", knowledgebase), which removes the usual remote-management tell (Supermicro IPMI vs Dell iDRAC vs HPE iLO). What is established: WebNX is a component-level in-house integrator — its job ad describes "assembling & disassembling servers, testing hardware," "repair or replace faulty hardware components," and moving "pallets full of servers or other computer parts with a pallet jack," and states WebNX tests "hundreds of server configurations and parts each year" (Indeed). The only text anywhere naming Dell / SuperMicro / HP describes GorillaServers — same founder (Daniel Pautz), same Ogden building, different legal entity — and comes from third-party review copy, not company disclosure. It must not be used to infer WebNX's fleet.
2 WebNX offers GPU servers CONFIRMED — the only working-list hardware claim that fully stands up Ogden alone lists 14 publicly priced GPU SKUs, from $299/mo (dual Xeon Gold 6148 with a GT 1030) to $7,999/mo (dual AMD EPYC 9575F, 1.5 TB DDR5, 2 × 15 TB Gen5 NVMe, 4 × RTX PRO 6000 96 GB). Separate GPU & AI store categories also exist for Los Angeles and Dallas. LinkedIn specialties independently list "ai server rental" and "hpc rental." (Ogden GPU & AI store, read 2026-08-03)
3 WebNX runs an Ogden, Utah datacenter CONFIRMED — four independent sources WebNX's own Utah datacenter page: Ogden facility over 100,000 sq ft, N+1 power (dual grid utility feeds, N+1 UPS, multiple diesel generators), direct uplinks to Level3, NTT, Zayo, Telia, China Unicom. DataCenterMap lists "WebNX Ogden Data Center, 119 North 600 West, Bldg 3B." BBB lists the company at that address. WebNX's own Indeed posting places DC technician roles there and states WebNX "operates self-managed data centers across Utah, California, and New York." The April 2021 generator fire during an Ogden city-wide power outage (The Register, 2021-04-06) further corroborates the physical site. (LinkedIn's "nearly 150,000 square-foot" is a size conflict, not a location conflict — see §15.)
4 Implicit premise: WebNX is an existing Supermicro customer CONTRADICTED as a premise The premise cannot be supported. WebNX is the provider in this research set with zero confirmed server-brand evidence of any kind. Opening a call with "we know you're running Supermicro" will fail on the first question. The correct posture is platform conversion with a discovery question, not incumbent defence.
5 Third-party listings (datacenters.com, ZoomInfo) place WebNX HQ in California CONTRADICTED — Ogden adopted BBB, both Indeed job postings and the CEO's own LinkedIn all place the company in Ogden, Utah. Ogden is where the CEO, the jobs and the BBB record sit. The California attribution is treated as data-vendor error or a reflection of the Equinix LA3 leased footprint. Unresolved at source — flagged, not silently dropped

4. Leadership & ownership

Evidence grades used in this section: primary-record = internet-number registry, court docket, campaign-finance filing, regulator profile, or the company's own published page · corroborated = two or more independent secondary sources agree · single-source = one secondary source only, no corroboration · GAP = nothing found after a named search.

Two framing facts before the table. First, the live 2026 webnx.com names nobody at all — it is a client-rendered React SPA whose /about, /contact and /company/the-team/ paths all serve an identical 2,166-byte shell, and whose schema.org Organization block carries the literal placeholder founder string "WebNX Founder". Every name below therefore comes from a registry, a filing, an archived capture of the company's own former team page, or a secondary source — never from the current site. Second, registry officers and network contacts are listed as rows in their own right and labelled as such: an ARIN OrgAdmin or an IRR admin-c is a named human being with a published, legitimately public route, and is often the only officer-grade name a private company exposes.

4.3 Buying committee

WebNX is a founder-owned private company with roughly 18 employees, no published CFO and no published board. There is effectively no procurement layer between the founder and a hardware purchase order — which shortens the path but also means the technical credibility of the first contact decides everything.

Name Why they matter for a server purchase How to approach
Daniel Pautz — President & CEO, Founder He is the signature. Three independent primary records make him the sole named principal: ARIN OrgAdmin under his personal email, BBB Principal Contact and Customer Contact, and "President and CEO" on the company's own team page for a decade. His published bio is explicitly hardware-centric — a self-taught hardware builder who developed his own line of whitebox servers — so he is technically opinionated about server specs, not merely a signer. He also owns GorillaServers in the same building, so one relationship can cover two AS numbers Direct, technical, specific. Email dan@webnx.com (published in ARIN, a legitimately public route) or engage him on WebHostingTalk where he posts as "dan". Lead with density, power envelope and delivery lead times against a named platform already in their live catalogue — AMD EPYC Genoa, Threadripper, Xeon E-series and GPU builds. Do not open with a generic company introduction; a founder who builds whitebox servers will bin it
Dario Perovich — Co-Founder & COO Co-founder and the only other person the company has ever named in an executive capacity — on its own site, on the BBB record, and as tech-c on the RADB IRR object. His own FEC filing self-describes his occupation as "MANAGEMENT". As COO of a colocation and dedicated-server operator he owns the deployment reality — rack space, power, cooling, staging labour — which is where a large server order either works or does not. He can kill a deal on operational grounds even if Pautz likes the box Second call, not first. Once Pautz is engaged, bring Perovich in on deployment mechanics: rack-and-stack labour, IPMI / out-of-band management, RMA turnaround, staging in Ogden versus the LA/Equinix and Manhattan sites. No published individual email — route via noc@webnx.com, a Pautz-brokered introduction, or LinkedIn
Mitch (Mitchell) Pautz — Director of Infrastructure The most senior named technical role below the founders and the closest thing WebNX has to a VP Infrastructure. Aggregator data places him in Glendora, CA, which maps to the ~10,000 sq ft Los Angeles footprint rather than the 100,000+ sq ft Ogden site — so he plausibly owns platform standards across sites. Shares a surname with the CEO, which suggests unusual internal trust; no filing confirms a family relationship, so that stays an inference, not a fact Technical validation track. LinkedIn is the only verified route — his email is aggregator-masked and must not be guessed. Engage on platform standardisation, GPU/HPC density and refresh cycles across Ogden + LA + Manhattan
Lynn Ure — V.P. Operations A VP-level operations owner distinct from the COO, and the LeadIQ data pattern suggests promotion from Operations Manager — i.e. deep institutional knowledge of how kit actually gets received and deployed. In a company this size the VP Ops is usually the person who converts an approved purchase into a delivery schedule and who feels the pain of a bad vendor Post-technical, pre-signature. LinkedIn. Talk delivery windows, palletisation, on-site spares and support SLA — operational risk reduction, not speeds and feeds
Jake Keitz — Operations Manager (Ogden) Day-to-day operations manager and a realistic early-signal contact: he will know actual refresh timing and what is failing in the field long before a purchase is formalised. Not a signer, but a credible internal advocate Low-key discovery. Aggregator-masked email only (j***@webnx.com), so reach him via the published support/NOC routes or LinkedIn. Anchor on the roles they are currently hiring — "Computer Tech - Data Center", "Logistics & Inventory Clerk", "Principal Linux Systems Engineer" — which together say they are building and racking servers in volume and staffing up receiving
Kek P. — Business Development (partial name — GAP) The only commercial/BD-side name attached to WebNX since Marc Rosenthal left, and only a first name plus initial is public. Flagged as an influence node rather than a target because the identification is single-source and incomplete Do not attempt contact by name until the surname is confirmed by a primary record. Use sales@webnx.com (1-800-840-5996 ext. 1) and ask for the business development lead by role, not by name

4.4 Unfilled roles — every one a GAP

GAP — CFO / VP Finance / Controller: no name, ever. WebNX has never published a CFO; the nearest historical equivalent, Marc Rosenthal ("Head of Business Development and Finance"), appears only in 2015 captures and was gone by 2019-08-21. Nobody has filled the slot publicly in 7+ years. · GAP — Accounts Payable / billing owner: only the role mailbox accounts@webnx.com and 1-800-840-5996 ext. 2. · GAP — Procurement / purchasing / vendor manager: no such title in any roster, job board, LinkedIn result or filing; the closest signal is an open "Logistics & Inventory Clerk" req in Ogden at $18–20/hr, implying receiving is being staffed but purchasing authority is not delegated. · GAP — CTO / VP Engineering: no name, and probably no such role — the senior technical title that does exist is "Director of Infrastructure", reinforced by current reqs for a "Hands-on Architect" and a "Principal Linux Systems Engineer", both explicitly individual-contributor, not leadership. · GAP — VP Sales / Head of Sales: only the partial "Kek P., Business Development". · GAP — corporate officers and directors of record: the Utah Division of Corporations search sits behind a Cloudflare interactive challenge; CAPTCHAs are not bypassed, so officers, directors, managers/members and annual-report signatories are unverified, and every aggregator mirror was blocked or empty. · GAP — registered agent for WebNX, Inc. · GAP — Utah entity number and filing history, including previously listed officers. · GAP — the "President" reported as initials "J. F." by ZoomInfo/LeadIQ: unresolved and conflicting with WebNX's own site, which gave "President and CEO" to Daniel Pautz through November 2024. Could be a genuine recent hire, a stale record, or an aggregator artefact — no name will be guessed. · GAP — GorillaServers, Inc. (AS53850) officers: its ARIN record is 100% role accounts; only Daniel Pautz's founder link is documented, and via press coverage rather than a filing. · GAP — UCC-1 secured parties and debtor signatories: the Utah UCC search sits on the same blocked state platform. · GAP — USPTO trademark declaration signatory and correspondent: no WEBNX trademark record was retrievable, and it is quite possible no federal registration exists. · GAP — historical pre-privacy webnx.com WHOIS registrant: every history provider reached was paywalled or bot-blocked. · GAP — Weber County owner of record for 119 N 600 W: not retrieved, and note it would name the landlord, not WebNX — the federal court's own findings state WebNX entered a long-term lease for the building in 2014 and bought the generator from its landlord.

4.5 Sources worked — including what returned nothing

Productive: ARIN Whois/RDAP (autnum 18450, Org WEBNX POC list, and a POC-by-email-domain search on @webnx.com) — returned exactly one human, Daniel Pautz, plus three role accounts; AS53850 (GorillaServers) returned four role accounts and zero personal names. · RADB IRR via bgp.he.net — the only registry record naming Perovich. · BBB business profile — confirms legal name, the 1999-04-04 establishment date, Pautz as Principal and Customer Contact, Perovich as COO, plus an unexplained "incorporated 2018-09-14" pointer. · Wayback Machine CDX + archived webnx.com — the single richest source: the 2015 /company/the-team/ captures carry three bios and are where Marc Rosenthal was recovered; 2019, 2021, 2023 and 2024 captures carry only Pautz and Perovich, pinning his departure to between late 2015 and mid-2019. · CourtListener / RECAP — 17 dockets; WebNX Inc v. Cummins Inc (D. Utah 1:23-cv-00055-JNP-JCB) was read in full. · FEC Schedule A — run on every name; the employer-matched search surfaced three otherwise-invisible staff and corroborated Perovich. · LinkedIn public profiles and RocketReach/LeadIQ/ZoomInfo (secondary, graded accordingly).

Reached but returned nothing on people: PeeringDB (net/4729 is populated on the network side but /api/poc returns an empty array; poc_updated stale since 2017-08-08) · the live webnx.com (the 1.7 MB JS bundle was pulled and searched directly: zero occurrences of Pautz, Perovich, Ure or Keitz, and a placeholder founder string) · historical WHOIS for webnx.com (registrant fully redacted behind Tucows tiered access; whoisrequest 403, whoxy 522, viewdns paid-only, securitytrails auth-required) · USPTO TSDR/TESS (API 404s, Justia 403 behind a JS challenge, no WEBNX mark surfaced at all) · job postings on Indeed, Glassdoor, KSL and ZipRecruiter (no named hiring manager, no "reports to" line, no recruiter name in any posting) · LowEndTalk (nothing; searches polluted by the unrelated webnx.in) · WebHostingTalk (thin — confirms only that Pautz posts as "dan") · conference speaker and exhibitor lists for HostingCon, CloudFest / CloudFest Americas, DataCenter World, NANOG and WHD.global (no WebNX record on any of them) · press, interviews and podcasts (the 2021 generator fire was covered by The Register, DCD, Data Center Frontier, TechRadar and Slashdot, but the only named source in that coverage is Kevin Brown, Ogden City Fire Marshal — an external official; Pautz is referenced as CEO but never quoted).

Blocked or not reached, and why: Utah Division of Corporations — secure.utah.gov/bes/ is retired and redirects to businessregistration.utah.gov, which returns HTTP 403 behind a Cloudflare interactive challenge; CAPTCHAs are not solved, so this is a hard stop, not an oversight. · Third-party Utah mirrors — OpenCorporates (CAPTCHA), Bizapedia (CAPTCHA), CorporationWiki (403), OpenGovUS (404), BizProfile (wrong entities), Buzzfile (not found). · Utah UCC search — same blocked platform. · Weber County property/assessor — connection failed, and deprioritised because the court record already establishes WebNX is a tenant. · Delaware and Nevada registries — not separately queried: every primary record reached puts the operating entity at 119 N 600 W, Ogden UT as a Utah corporation, so pursuing DE/NV without a lead would have been speculative.

Best neutral next step to close the officer gap without touching the company: Paul T. Note the firm is a property-subrogation practice, which implies WebNX's insurer is driving that recovery.

6. Hardware fleet

Evidence grades used here: confirmed = first-hand named disclosure or multiple independent corroboration · circumstantial = behaviour points strongly but the vendor is never named · inferred = derived only from CPU socket or chassis form factor · contradicted = evidence runs the other way · GAP = nothing found.

Vendor / category Evidence grade What the evidence actually says
Whitebox / in-house custom integration CONFIRMED — and this is the most accurate characterisation of the fleet WebNX's own Indeed posting for a Computer Tech at 119 North 600 West, Ogden ($15–17/hr) lists "assembling & disassembling servers, testing hardware, updating documentation, and setting up server racks with cables," "repair or replace faulty hardware components," "sort, test, and organize computer hardware," and moving "pallets full of servers or other computer parts with a pallet jack." The same posting states WebNX tests "hundreds of server configurations and parts each year." WebNX markets "top-quality components from top manufacturers" and describes itself on LinkedIn as building "high end custom dedicated servers" with the tagline "If you can dream it we have likely built it." A 7-year WebHostingTalk customer review describes swapping hardware 4–5 times by simply requesting a config and receiving a quote within days — behaviour consistent with an in-house integrator holding component inventory, not an OEM-order-cycle buyer
NVIDIA (direct appliance) CONFIRMED Verbatim from the Ogden GPU & AI store: "(GPU-OGD) NVIDIA Spark Blackwell DGX Super Computer, 128GB VRAM, 4TB NVMe, 10G" at $459.00/month, and "(GPU-OGD) 2X NVIDIA Spark DGX + 2 x 200Gbps Direct Connect" at $1,099.00/month. NVIDIA DGX Spark = Blackwell, 20-core ARM, 128 GB unified VRAM, 4 TB Gen4 NVMe. This proves at least part of the AI fleet is bought as finished vendor-branded systems, and the 200 Gbps direct-connect pairing indicates they are building small multi-node AI clusters
Supermicro UNVERIFIED / NOT CONFIRMED — the key open question for this account. Never state it as fact. No Supermicro SKU, chassis model or motherboard model appears anywhere in WebNX's public product pages, job ads, datacenter pages, forum posts or PeeringDB record. Three adjacent signals only, all explicitly non-probative: (1) every WebNX product description lists CPU/RAM/NVMe/GPU/network and never a chassis or system vendor; (2) adjacent entity only — GorillaServers, Inc., a legally separate company founded by the same person in the same Ogden facility and which per LowEndTalk community posts "sometimes purchases equipment from WebNX", is described in third-party reviews as offering "only modern servers based on Dell, SuperMicro, and HP" with "all servers hosted on reliable SuperMicro platforms" — that is community/third-party text about a different legal entity (LowEndTalk thread); (3) Supermicro is the only tier-1 vendor shipping server boards for the desktop-class sockets WebNX actually sells (AM5 Ryzen 9950X/9950X3D, LGA1851 Core Ultra 9 285K), which raises the prior but proves nothing
Cisco (network layer) — ASR 9000 series routers + Noction IRP CIRCUMSTANTIAL — facility-adjacent, community-sourced Attribution caveat: this hardware list appears in the GorillaServers LowEndTalk marketing post describing the shared Ogden facility ("Multiple Cisco ASR 9000 Routers," "Fully Redundant UPS & Diesel Generators," "Noiction IRP – Intelligent Routing Platform") — not in a WebNX-authored document (LowEndTalk). WebNX independently markets a "BGP route-optimized network," which is consistent with a Noction-style IRP on carrier-class routers
Dell / HPE GAP for WebNX No PowerEdge or ProLiant model is named on any WebNX page. Targeted searches for "WebNX" + PowerEdge / ProLiant / iDRAC / iLO returned no WebNX-specific result. The WHMCS knowledgebase is empty (knowledgebase), removing the usual route to confirm remote-management branding — normally the most reliable vendor tell. The only Dell/HP linkage anywhere is the third-party GorillaServers review text describing a separate company

CPU generations observed, and what they imply about fleet age

Read directly off the publicly sold platform mix (this is inferred platform-family evidence from published configurations, not vendor disclosure):

  • Current generation: AMD Ryzen 9950X / 9950X3D (AM5 desktop socket), AMD Threadripper, dual AMD EPYC 9575F (128C/256T, Turin, 1.5 TB DDR5), Intel Core Ultra 9 285K / Ultra 7 265K / Ultra 5 250K (LGA1851 desktop socket)
  • Mid-life: AMD EPYC 7443P (single-socket Milan, 24C/48T) — appears across many GPU SKUs as the standard GPU host
  • Legacy: dual Intel Xeon Gold 6148 and 6152 (Skylake-SP, 2017), dual Intel Xeon E5-2690v4 and E5-2630v4 (Broadwell, 2016) in 12-bay storage nodes
  • Chassis form factors inferred: 12-bay 3.5" storage chassis (12 × 26 TB / 12 × 28 TB HDD + 480 GB SSD, "HW RAID or JBOD") and 4U-class 4-GPU chassis (4 × RTX 3090 / 4 × V100 / 4 × RTX PRO 6000 96 GB)

What this implies: the fleet is barbelled, not uniformly old. WebNX is simultaneously running 2016–2017 Broadwell/Skylake storage and GPU-host nodes and buying dual-Turin Blackwell platforms in the same catalogue. That is the signature of an integrator that refreshes opportunistically at component level rather than on an OEM fleet cycle — and it means the realistic Supermicro entry point is barebones chassis, motherboards and GPU-optimised 4U/5U systems, not fully-configured OEM SKUs.

8. Financing chain & UCC record

Scope of this track: WebNX, Inc. (webnx.com, AS18450) — Ogden, Utah; whitebox dedicated-server / colocation operator. Sister entity checked in parallel: GorillaServers, Inc. (gorillaservers.com), same Ogden building, WebNX is its registrar of record.

8.1 Verdict

UNVERIFIED — portal blocked

Read that exactly as written. Zero UCC filings were retrieved for either debtor, and no debtor-name query was ever actually submitted to any Utah UCC system — every attempt failed at the authentication or routing layer before a search field was reachable. The empty filing set in §8.3 means nothing was seen. It does not mean WebNX has no secured debt. It must never be reported to a customer, quoted to credit, or entered into CRM as "no liens found". The gating here is procedural, not evidence of a clean balance sheet.

8.4 What the record means

Inference, not sighted evidence. Every row states its own confidence.

Observation What it implies Confidence Sales consequence
The Utah UCC debtor search is not a public record in the browsable sense — it sits entirely behind a Utah-ID SSO login (ucc.utah.gov serves only a password form; all 13 candidate search paths 404). The only anonymous search Utah exposes on that host is an entity/trademark search whose heading still literally reads "Nevada Business Search". The correct reading of this track is: WebNX's lien position is UNKNOWN, not clean. No conclusion about WebNX's secured debt can be drawn from the absence of search results. Any representation that "WebNX has no liens" would be unfounded. The gating is procedural, not evidence of a clean balance sheet. high — directly observed: HTTP 200 login gate with only __RequestVerificationToken / password / ErrorMessage inputs, and 13/13 404s on UCC search paths Do NOT let anyone build a credit or channel-approval argument on "no UCC filings found". Spend $24.00 and ~10 working days on two UCC-11 "Information Request (National)" forms (WEBNX, INC. and GORILLASERVERS, INC.) before any Rule 8 distributor-approval conversation or direct-terms decision. That is the cheapest de-risking step in this entire dossier.
WebNX publicly states, in its own WHT colocation offer dated 2025-01-24, "in some cases we can also offer high end and mid level LTO on servers (we keep millions in hardware on hand)". LTO = lease-to-own. They are extending equipment financing TO their own customers. (WHT thread 1933969) An operator that originates lease-to-own paper on hardware is either (a) funding that inventory from cash/retained earnings, or (b) funding it on a floor-plan/inventory line — and a floor-plan line is almost always perfected by a UCC-1 blanket filing on inventory, accounts and proceeds. "Millions in hardware on hand" is exactly the collateral pool a lender would file against. This materially raises the prior that at least one active UCC-1 exists, which the blocked portal simply did not let us see. medium — the LTO / "millions in hardware" quote is directly sourced and dated; the inference to a floor-plan lien is commercial reasoning, not a sighted filing Assume a blanket lien may exist until the certified search says otherwise. If a blanket UCC-1 on "inventory, equipment, accounts and proceeds" turns up, a new vendor cannot take a purchase-money security interest in shipped hardware without a PMSI notice to the prior secured party — a real deal-timing risk. Ask credit to plan for PMSI notice mechanics now, rather than discovering it at PO stage.
WebNX buys and racks whitebox parts, not vendor-branded systems: the observed fleet is Supermicro-class boards plus loose AMD EPYC / Ryzen / Threadripper CPUs, Kioxia / Samsung U.2 NVMe, and consumer NVIDIA GeForce cards (RTX 3090 / 4090 / 5090) alongside datacenter A100 / RTX 6000. No Dell / HPE / Lenovo SKU pattern appears anywhere in 862 harvested catalogue records. If a secured party is ever found, its identity is the tell. A bank or equipment lessor (Zions, Celtic, a regional Utah bank, or a lessor such as Meridian/Trinity) implies WebNX buys direct with its own working capital and self-integrates — the profile of a buyer you can sell components/platforms to directly. A distributor or vendor-finance arm implies they already transact through a channel, which is what actually bears on Rule 8 distributor approval. A whitebox integrator with zero brand-system footprint most often shows up with a bank or an independent lessor, not a vendor captive. medium — the whitebox hardware pattern is strongly evidenced across the catalogue; the mapping from secured-party type to buying route is industry inference The single most commercially useful field on the certified search is the secured party NAME. Read it first. If it is a distributor or vendor captive, route the deal through that existing channel and Rule 8 approval is largely pre-solved. If it is a bank or an independent lessor, they are a direct buyer and you should pitch direct terms — but you must then clear the blanket-lien / PMSI issue above.
No filing dates could be obtained, so filing-date cadence cannot be read from the register. However, an independent purchase clock was reconstructed from the catalogue (see §9): eight silicon-generation purchase events between 2023-04 and 2025-10 at a 2.35-month median interval, then a hard stop — zero new CPU or GPU generations in the ten months since 2025-10-12, across well-covered 2026 snapshots. If liens exist and their filing dates cluster in 2024–2025 and then stop in late 2025, that would corroborate a financed buying programme that has been paused or exhausted. If instead filings are old and continuing (continuations rather than new filings), it implies legacy debt still outstanding against an operator that has stopped refreshing — the capital-constrained reading. Either way the register would date the financing, which is why the dates matter as much as the names. medium — the purchase-clock stop is directly observed and well-covered; its linkage to financing is inference When the certified search returns, compare filing dates against the 2025-10-12 purchase-clock stop. Alignment converts a soft "they paused buying" story into a hard "their financing window closed" story, which changes the pitch from product to financing structure.
Utah's register indexes UCC and CFS (the Central Filing System, i.e. the agricultural/farm-products register) together, and the search is by debtor name or filing number, one name per UCC-11 form. A single form covering WEBNX, INC. will not catch filings indexed against alias or predecessor strings, nor against GorillaServers. Utah UCC search logic is exact/standard-search based, so WEBNX INC vs WEBNX, INC. vs WEB NX can return different sets. A one-form search is a false negative waiting to happen. high — "One name or file number per form" is quoted verbatim from the Division's own fee page Budget for four forms, not one: WEBNX, INC., WEBNX INC, GORILLASERVERS, INC., GORILLASERVERS INC = $48.00. Cheap insurance against a false "no filings" result that would then get repeated inside the CRM as fact.
GorillaServers, Inc. operates from the same Ogden building and WebNX is its domain registrar of record, but the two run entirely separate WHMCS storefronts, separate brands and separate PoP lists (GorillaServers advertises Ogden, LA, Dallas, Staten Island — the same four metros WebNX now sells). These are almost certainly commonly-controlled or tightly-affiliated entities sharing floor space, network (AS18450) and procurement. If financing exists it may sit on whichever entity holds the hardware, and a search against only WebNX could miss a lien filed against GorillaServers over the very same physical fleet. Conversely, if only one entity carries liens, that tells you which entity is the balance-sheet vehicle — and therefore which one your contract and credit exposure should name. medium — co-location, shared registrar and identical four-metro PoP footprint are observed; common control is inferred, not verified from an ownership record Search BOTH debtors and compare. Whichever entity carries the secured debt is the real balance sheet — contract with that one, or insist on a guarantee from it. Also verify officer overlap via the Utah entity search (free, and reachable in a human browser) before extending terms to either.
If the certified search comes back genuinely empty for both entities, that is itself a strong commercial signal for an operator running thousands of servers across five metros (Ogden 100k–120k sq ft owned/run, 200+ racks and 5 cages at Equinix LA3, plus LA1, NY1, NY2, DA1). A fleet that size with zero perfected liens means one of three things: (1) cash-funded from operating margin — very plausible for a 1999-founded, founder-run, debt-averse hosting business, implying real free cash flow and no lender veto; (2) operating leases structured so no UCC-1 is filed, or hardware held off balance sheet; or (3) financing filed in another jurisdiction against a differently-named entity. Option (1) is the most likely for a business that itself originates lease-to-own paper to customers — you generally do not lend when you are borrowed to the hilt. medium — conditional inference; cannot be tested until the certified search returns A clean, verified "no filings" result is a GREEN light for direct terms and larger deal sizes: no lender consent, no PMSI notice, no intercreditor friction, and a counterparty that has been self-funding capex. It would also mean price, not financing, is the lever — which matters because §10 shows their 24-month payback math is currently broken by memory prices.
With multiple secured parties, Utah priority runs by first-to-file-or-perfect under UCC Article 9, with a purchase-money security interest in equipment taking super-priority only if perfected within 20 days of the debtor receiving possession and, for inventory, only with advance written notice to prior filers. If the search returns two or more active filings, the earliest-filed blanket lien controls the collateral pool. A later vendor cannot simply out-file it; a PMSI must be affirmatively perfected and noticed on a tight clock. high — settled Article 9 mechanics; Utah's secured-transactions chapter is Title 70A Chapter 9a If two or more filings appear, do not quote payment terms until credit has read the collateral descriptions and confirmed whether a PMSI is available. The 20-day equipment clock means the paperwork has to be ready BEFORE hardware ships, not after — build that into the quote-to-ship process or the security interest is lost.
A termination statement, if found, would mean the underlying obligation was satisfied and the collateral released; a continuation (filed within the six months before the five-year lapse) would mean the debt is still live. Terminations restore unencumbered borrowing capacity — WebNX would be free to pledge the fleet again, i.e. able to finance a large catch-up hardware buy. Active continuations mean the collateral is still committed and incremental capex likely has to be cash-funded or subordinated. high — standard Article 9 lapse/continuation mechanics; directly readable off the certified search once obtained This is the field that tells you whether a vendor-financed refresh is even possible. Given the purchase clock stopped 2025-10-12 and current memory prices make a cash-funded refresh uneconomic (§10), a recent TERMINATION would be the single best buying signal in this file — free collateral, plus an overdue refresh, plus broken cash-payback math, equals a financed-deal opening.

8.5 GAP — Track 1, stated plainly

  • UCC IS UNVERIFIED, NOT CLEAN. No Utah UCC filing was retrieved for either debtor. The register sits entirely behind a Utah-ID SSO login (ucc.utah.gov returns only a password form; 13 of 13 candidate UCC search paths return 404). No account was created and no credentials were entered. §8.3 is empty because nothing was seen — this must never be reported as "no financing." Resolution: UCC-11 "Information Request (National)", $12.00 per name, ~10 working days, by mail/fax/in person. Recommended: four forms ($48.00) — WEBNX, INC., WEBNX INC, GORILLASERVERS, INC., GORILLASERVERS INC.
  • No debtor-name query was ever actually submitted to any Utah UCC system. Every attempt failed at the authentication or routing layer before a search field was reachable. The alias variants listed in §8.2 were the strings intended to be submitted, not strings that were run against the register. Do not read them as executed searches.
  • businessregistration.utah.gov (the redirect target of secure.utah.gov/bes named in the brief) returns HTTP 403 behind a Cloudflare challenge to automated access. A human browser would very likely load it fine. Utah entity records for WebNX, Inc. and GorillaServers, Inc. — registered agent, officers, status, formation date — were therefore NOT retrieved, so officer overlap between the two entities is asserted as plausible but is UNVERIFIED. (This is the same hard stop already recorded in §4.4.)
  • Common control between WebNX, Inc. and GorillaServers, Inc. is inferred, not documented — from shared Ogden premises, WebNX being registrar of record for gorillaservers.com, and an identical four-metro PoP footprint. No ownership document, filing or officer list was obtained to confirm it.

9.2 Cadence

Observed intervals — measured between first-appearance dates of new CPU/GPU generations in the catalogue:

From → to Interval
2023-04-22 → 2024-07-15 14.8 months — archive coverage is thin across 2023-06 to 2024-03, so this is an upper bound and probably conceals one or more events
2024-07-15 → 2024-09-20 2.2 months
2024-09-20 → 2024-12-05 2.5 months
2024-12-05 → 2025-03-19 3.4 months
2025-03-19 → 2025-04-27 1.3 months
2025-04-27 → 2025-05-17 0.7 months
2025-05-17 → 2025-08-05 2.6 months
2025-08-05 → 2025-10-12 2.2 months
2025-10-12 → today (2026-08-10) 9.9 months with NO purchase event

Median months: 2.35 months (median of the eight observed intervals: 0.7, 1.3, 2.2, 2.2, 2.5, 2.6, 3.4, 14.8). Excluding the coverage-limited 14.8-month interval, the median of the seven clean 2024–2025 intervals is 2.2 months.

Last purchase event: 2025-10-12 — simultaneous first appearance of AMD Threadripper 9000 (Zen 5, 9960X/9970X), AMD EPYC 9255, and the AMD EPYC 4004/4005 AM5 line (4585PX), observed at web.archive.org 2025-10-12.

Next window estimate: OVERDUE. On the observed 2.2–2.35-month cadence the next purchase event was due around 2025-12-20; it is now roughly 8 months late, and four clean 2026 snapshot windows (Jan, Feb, Mar/Apr, May) plus today's live catalogue show no new silicon. The estimate is that the next genuine purchase event lands in Q4 2026 – Q1 2027, and that it is gated on DRAM pricing rather than on silicon availability. Two concrete triggers to watch: (1) DDR5 RDIMM spot pricing falling back under roughly $700–900 per 64 GB module, which multiple 2026 sources put in 2027 rather than 2026; (2) availability of vendor or lender financing that lets them buy without funding memory from cash. A third, weaker trigger is AMD's next EPYC generation (Venice / Zen 6) creating competitive pressure they cannot ignore.

Basis: purchase events derived from 862 catalogue records — 681 products from 134 archived WHMCS store-category snapshots, 105 priced table rows from 80 archived WordPress catalogue pages, and 57 live SKUs from the WebNX product API on 2026-08-10. Cross-corroborated against WebNX's own dated WebHostingTalk announcements (2024-11-30 EPYC 9005 launch with "9255… stock due in middle of month"; 2025-06-27 "Lots of newest generation EPYCs in stock, from 9275F to 9755 duals"). The 2026 no-event finding is supported by good archive coverage (52 category pages across 6 distinct capture days in 2026-T1 alone) plus the live API, so it reflects real behaviour rather than a gap in the record.

Confidence: Medium-high on the 2024–2025 cadence (dense multi-source coverage, corroborated by the operator's own dated posts). Medium-high on the fact of the 2025-10 stop (good 2026 coverage from two independent surfaces). Medium on the forward window, which depends on a DRAM price path that is a forecast, not an observation. Low on the single 2023–2024 interval, which is coverage-limited and should not be treated as a real 15-month gap.

9.4 GAP — Track 2 and cross-track, stated plainly

  • SKU COUNT CONFLICT WITH THE BRIEF. The brief states 1,851 live SKUs. That figure could not be reproduced from any surface. The live WebNX product API (POST webnx.com/api/whmcs/getProducts) returns result:'success', totalResults:57. Scraping all 23 live WHMCS store categories yields 74 product listings including 8 colocation items. The widest catalogue ever observed in the archive is 141 distinct SKUs (2025-10-12). 1,851 may count configurable build permutations, upgrade options, historical/retired products, or a different vendor's data — but it does not match WebNX's live catalogue as published. Treat 57 in-stock / 74 listed as the measured figures and reconcile the 1,851 before it is used commercially.
  • RACK COUNT CONFLICT WITH THE BRIEF. The brief states ~150 racks at Equinix LA3. WebNX's own dated statement (2025-01-24) is "one of the largest clients of Equinix LA3, now over 200 racks and 5 cages". That is a self-reported marketing claim, not an audited figure, and it is 18 months old. Neither 150 nor 200+ is independently verified. PeeringDB does not even list LA3 as a WebNX facility (it lists Equinix LA1), so PeeringDB cannot corroborate either number. (This supersedes nothing in §5 — both figures remain unresolved.)
  • The webnx.com marketing catalogue is a client-side React SPA whose page shell is only ~2.1 KB. Wayback captured only the empty shell for /all-dedicated-servers and /standard-dedicated-servers, so historical SKU data could NOT be recovered from the marketing site at all. The entire timeline rests on the server-rendered WHMCS store plus the pre-2025 WordPress catalogue. Any SKU that existed only on the marketing site is invisible to this study.
  • 2023 archive coverage is thin (1 WHMCS store capture in Oct 2023, 1 in Dec 2023; WordPress pages carry that year). The apparent 14.8-month interval between the 2023-04-22 and 2024-07-15 purchase events is very likely a coverage artifact concealing one or more real events, and it was excluded from the tightened median for that reason. Do not cite a 15-month historical purchase gap as fact.
  • Per-snapshot SKU counts are archive-coverage dependent, not catalogue censuses. A snapshot window with 32 captured category pages will naturally show more distinct SKUs than one with 4. Compare the trend and the silicon, not the raw counts — except where noted (2025-10 and 2026-02 both had strong coverage, which is what makes the fall to 57 live meaningful).
  • Exact launch dates for the DA1 Dallas and NY2 Staten Island PoPs were NOT established. Both datacenter pages (webnx.com/datacenter-locations-and-networks/dallas and /statenisland) have zero Wayback captures and are JS-rendered, so no dated content could be extracted. Dallas is bounded only as live by 2026-04-25; Staten Island has never been archived and is bounded only as live by today. The NYIIX 100G peering created 2025-04-16 is the nearest dated proxy for the NY expansion.
  • WebNX's WHMCS announcements page renders an "Aug 2026" month header but returns no announcement items, and no working RSS feed was found (three candidate endpoints tried). Any dated product announcements from 2026 are therefore missing from the signal set, which weakens confidence in the "no purchase event in 2026" finding at the margin — though the catalogue evidence for it is strong and independent.
  • No WebHostingTalk post by WebNX later than 2025-06-27 was located. WHT's member post-history search returned HTTP 403 to automated access, so their full thread list could not be enumerated. A 2026 WHT announcement may exist and would be the fastest way to confirm or refute the purchase-clock stop. Worth one manual check.
  • RTX 3090 shows a first-appearance date of 2026-08-10 in the pattern scan, which is an artifact — 3090-class cards are referenced in WHT threads from 2022 and were certainly racked long before. Treat GPU first-appearance dates as less reliable than CPU dates, because GPUs frequently appear as configurable upgrades rather than as named catalogue SKUs.
  • CROSS-TRACK — PeeringDB facility data for AS18450 is stale: the most recent facility added is Equinix LA1 in 2019, and the record's last general update was 2022-07-27. Equinix LA3, DA1 Dallas and NY2 Staten Island are all absent. PeeringDB therefore understates the current footprint and must not be used alone to size this account.
  • CROSS-TRACK — Company scale figures were not established: employee count, revenue, server count and exact Ogden square footage. WebNX self-describes the Utah facility as "over 100,000 sq ft" in one place and "Large 120k Sq" in another; both are self-reported marketing claims. No financial statements are available for this private company.

10. Cost ceiling

What each server can be worth to WebNX, and what the same box costs to build today. This section answers one question: at what hardware cost does a WebNX SKU still pay for itself?

10.1 Assumptions — read these first, they are assumptions and not findings

OPERATING-COST DEDUCTION: 40% of gross monthly rent, applied uniformly. Ceiling = monthly_price × (1 − 0.40) × N months. THIS IS AN ASSUMPTION, NOT A RESEARCHED FACT — it is an industry-norm band selected for this analysis, not something WebNX disclosed. Treat every ceiling figure below as a modelled estimate.

  • Assumption band. 30% (low) to 50% (high) is the defensible range for power + bandwidth + rack + remote hands + IP + churn/idle allowance on a single dedicated server. At 30% opex the 24-month ceilings rise by 17% versus the figures given; at 50% they fall by 17%. Applied to the EPYC 9255 SKU, the 24-month ceiling spans $10,062 (30%) / $8,626 (40%, used) / $7,188 (50%).
  • Why 40% is the midpoint, and the one real anchor. WebNX published its own retail colocation rate card on 2025-01-24 (WHT thread 1933969): a 1U/2U single server with 2 Amps at 208V (~400W), a /29, a 10G port and 50 TB of bandwidth retails at $129/month; a full rack with 3 KW usable, 100G and 100 TB is $1,500/month, with extra power at $300/kW; a private 25-rack cage with 75 KW pooled is $23,000/month. That $129 is a retail price to a third party and therefore an upper bound on WebNX's own internal cost to house one machine — their true internal cost is materially lower, particularly in Ogden where they operate their own ~100,000–120,000 sq ft facility rather than renting. Against a $259/mo dedicated SKU that retail-equivalent housing cost is ~50%; against a $599/mo SKU it is ~22%. 40% sits between those and is deliberately conservative.
  • Site asymmetry — real, but not modelled. Ogden is owner-operated, so opex there should sit at the low end of the band. Los Angeles is leased space at Equinix LA3, where WebNX self-describes as "one of the largest clients… now over 200 racks and 5 cages" on a recently renewed 5-year term; opex there belongs at the high end. A single 40% was applied to all sites for comparability. A site-split model would raise Ogden ceilings and lower LA ceilings by roughly 10 percentage points each.
  • What is excluded. No cost of capital / discounting (a 24-month payback at a realistic 10–12% cost of capital is ~8–10% more demanding than the undiscounted figure shown); no sales, support, licensing or overhead allocation; no allowance for the fact that stocklevel=1 on 26 of 57 live SKUs implies these are one-off builds without volume procurement leverage; no residual-value credit at end of life, which for used-Xeon and storage chassis is genuinely non-trivial and would raise the effective ceiling.
  • Payback-window choice. 12 / 18 / 24 months. Note that dedicated-server hardware in this segment is routinely held 4–6 years — the 2023 catalogue was still selling Xeon E5-2696v2 (2013 silicon) — so a 24-month payback is a commercial hurdle rate, not an asset-life constraint. Read the 24-month column as "the price at which this SKU is comfortably profitable", not "the price above which it is impossible".

10.4 Roll-up — and what the gap means for the pitch

HEADLINE: at today's street prices, WebNX's flagship current-gen EPYC SKU cannot be rebuilt at anything close to its own asking price. The gap is entirely memory.

SKU A — pid 587, AMD EPYC 9255 / 768 GB DDR5 / 2 × 3.84 TB NVMe, $599/mo, Ogden. Assembled component cost at 2026-08-10 street: $34,637.48 (CPU $2,835.00 + RAM $24,768.00 + NVMe $5,140.00 + board $649.00 + NIC $257.49 + chassis/PSU $987.99). Rent-derived 24-month ceiling: $8,626. BOM is 4.02× the ceiling. Undiscounted payback at 40% opex: 96.4 months — eight years. Memory alone is $24,768, which is 287% of the entire 24-month ceiling; the 768 GB of DDR5 costs 2.9× what the whole server can generate in two years. Now the same build at pre-crisis component prices (64 GB DDR5 RDIMM at ~$255, 3.84 TB NVMe at ~$500, everything else unchanged): $8,789.48 — 1.02× the 24-month ceiling, payback 24.5 months. That is a textbook, healthy whitebox build. So the $599 price point was set in a world where the machine paid for itself in ~24 months, and that world ended. Roughly $255 → $2,064 per 64 GB module is an ~8× move.

SKU B — pid 884, Ryzen 9950X / 128 GB / 7.6 TB / RTX 5090, $479/mo, Los Angeles. Assembled component cost: $11,679.47 (CPU $499.99 + RAM $1,094.00 + NVMe $5,140.00 + GPU $3,400.00 + board ~$300 + NIC $257.49 + chassis/PSU $987.99). 24-month ceiling: $6,898. BOM is 1.69×. Payback 40.6 months. The RTX 5090 alone at $3,400 is 49% of the 24-month ceiling — and it is a consumer card with no ECC, sold into an AI-inference use case. Note the compounding squeeze: GPU street price is running ~70% above the $1,999 MSRP because of GDDR7 costs, so this SKU is hit by the same DRAM shortage twice, once in system memory and once in the GPU.

SKU C — pid 942, Dual Xeon Gold 6226 / 192 GB DDR4 / 1.2 TB NVMe, $259/mo, Los Angeles. Assembled component cost: $2,467.03 (CPUs $147.84 + RAM $1,211.70 + NVMe ~$200 + board ~$350 + NIC $257.49 + chassis/PSU ~$300). 24-month ceiling: $3,730. BOM is 0.66× — the ONLY one of the three that clears its ceiling. Payback 15.9 months. The two 12-core Xeons cost $147.84, less than the network card. Even here, refurbished DDR4 is 49% of the BOM.

What the gap means for pitching an integrated platform against self-assembly:

  1. Do not lead with integration savings on chassis, boards or CPUs. Those line items are $1,900–2,000 combined on a $34,637 build — under 6%. A whitebox operator with its own hands in Ogden and Flex Space at LA3 will always beat an integrator on assembly labour, and they know it. Any pitch built on "we save you integration effort" loses on arithmetic.
  2. Lead with memory procurement, because that is where 71% of the current-gen BOM sits. The credible value propositions are contracted DDR5 allocation at below-spot pricing, a firm forward price, or memory-inclusive system pricing that shelters them from spot volatility. An integrated platform that bundles memory at contract rather than spot is worth multiples of every other saving combined.
  3. The self-assembly route is now actively worse than it used to be for them, and that is the opening. A whitebox builder's structural edge is buying components on the open market — which is precisely the market that broke. A vendor with allocation has a temporary, real advantage over a spot buyer for the first time in this company's 27-year history.
  4. Match the pitch to the tier. The used-Xeon tier (SKU C) still works economically and needs nothing from us. The current-gen EPYC tier (SKU A) is where the model is broken and where the conversation should start. The GPU tier (SKU B) is the middle case — squeezed but survivable.
  5. Expect a financing conversation, not just a price conversation. At 4.02× the 24-month ceiling, no rational operator funds SKU A from cash — which is exactly why the purchase clock stopped on 2025-10-12 (§9) and why the live catalogue has fallen from 141 SKUs to 57. The deal that unlocks this account is most likely structured (extended terms, lease, or memory-inclusive bundling), which is why the UCC lien position in §8 must be cleared first — it determines whether structured terms are even available.
  6. Sanity-check on their pricing power: at spot memory, a rebuilt SKU A would need roughly $2,400/mo to hit a 24-month payback versus the $599 they charge today. They are not going to quadruple list prices. Something else has to give — allocation, financing, or configuration (smaller memory footprints per box). Watch for memory downsizing in new SKUs as the leading indicator of which lever they chose.

10.5 GAP — Track 3, stated plainly

  • All prices are RETAIL/SPOT street prices as of 2026-08-10, sourced from public listings and price trackers. WebNX buys at distributor or contract pricing, which for a buyer of their volume is plausibly 15–40% below these figures on CPUs and drives, and potentially far below on memory if they hold allocation. Every BOM total is therefore an UPPER BOUND. The direction of the conclusion is robust (memory dominates; SKU A cannot pay back in 24 months at spot) but the magnitudes would compress under real contract pricing.
  • Memory pricing is extraordinarily volatile right now and the sources conflict. Datacenterdisk reports the cheapest 64 GB DDR5 ECC RDIMM at $2,064.00 ($32.25/GB) on 2026-08-10; other 2026 sources quote 64 GB RDIMMs at $1,200–$2,300, and a March 2026 reference put DDR5 ECC RDIMM at $520–$1,150. The tracker figure was used because it is dated and specific, but the BOM for SKU A swings by roughly $10,000 across that source range. This single input drives the headline conclusion and deserves a same-day re-check before the number is quoted to a customer.
  • The pre-crisis comparison for SKU A uses $255 per 64 GB DDR5 RDIMM and $500 per 3.84 TB NVMe. The $255 figure is taken from a secondary reference describing the Q3 2025 → early 2026 move (~$255 to over $900); the $500 NVMe figure is an estimate and is NOT sourced. The pre-crisis payback of 24.5 months should be treated as indicative, not precise.
  • Four BOM line items are UNSOURCED ESTIMATES and are flagged as such in §10.3: the AM5 motherboard for SKU B ($300), and for SKU C the 1.2 TB NVMe ($200), the dual LGA3647 motherboard ($350), and the used 2U chassis/PSU ($300). eBay listings for the Supermicro X11DPi-N were located but no confirmed transacted price was retrievable. Combined these total $1,150 and materially affect SKU C's roll-up (46% of its $2,467 BOM), so SKU C's 0.66× ratio is the least reliable of the three.
  • No firm street price was obtained for a single 7.68 TB U.2 NVMe Gen4 drive (the datacenterdisk URL for it returned 404). SKU B's storage is priced as 2 × 3.84 TB, which may overstate cost if WebNX uses one larger drive. Also note a conflict within the same source family on the 3.84 TB Kioxia CD8: a search snippet cited $999.99 / $260.41 per TB while the live page fetch on 2026-08-10 returned $2,570.00 / $669.27 per TB. The live fetch was used. This is a 2.6× discrepancy on a component appearing in two of three BOMs.
  • The RTX 5090 price of $3,400 is a midpoint of an observed $2,999–$3,700 August 2026 range, not a single quoted SKU price. Liquid-cooled variants exceed $5,000 and one source cited $4,329 on Amazon. WebNX's actual card model is not disclosed.
  • The 40% operating-cost deduction is an ASSUMPTION, not a researched fact, as flagged in §10.1. It is not derived from WebNX cost data, which is not public. The only real anchor is WebNX's own retail colo rate card, which bounds housing cost from above but does not reveal internal cost. All ceiling figures move roughly ±17% across the 30–50% band.
  • Ceilings are undiscounted and ignore cost of capital, residual value, licensing, support and sales overhead. A 24-month payback at a realistic 10–12% cost of capital is ~8–10% more demanding than shown.

11. Customers & network

Named customers

None. Zero named customers, zero logo wall, zero case studies, zero press releases naming clients. This is a hard GAP, not an oversight in the research.

Relationship Grade What the source actually says
GorillaServers, Inc. — equipment purchaser / adjacent operator Community-sourced, and contested within the source itself LowEndTalk participants state the two companies are "completely separate" and that "GorillaServers sometimes purchases equipment from WebNX," and separately that "WebNX is their own supplier… they colocate/own everything, they don't resell anything. If you want their stuff, you have to buy from them or Gorilla." GorillaServers runs its own ASN (AS53850), operates in the same 100,000+ sq ft Ogden facility, and was founded by the same person. Treat as a hardware/facility relationship, not a verified customer contract (LowEndTalk)
QuickPacket — facility neighbour Community-sourced; not a customer A LowEndTalk post notes "QuickPacket is in Equinix LA3, the same facility as WebNX." This establishes co-location proximity only — no evidence of any commercial relationship
Segment inference Inferred from SKU mix and IP data, not from disclosure ~31,446 domains across 1,707 IPs on AS18450 (IPinfo, via search snippet) indicates a long tail of hosting/reseller/SMB tenants rather than a few named anchor enterprises. The SKU mix — unmetered bandwidth, 12 × 28 TB storage nodes, 100 TB outbound allowances — plus LowEndTalk threads titled "Regarding WebNX distributors" and "looking gorillaservers reseller?" points to hosting resellers, CDN/streaming, backup/storage, and AI/ML hobbyist-to-startup tenants (bgpview.io/asn/18450)

Network — AS18450

  • Registry: AS18450 (WebNX, Inc.), registered 2007-07-27; IRR record RADB::AS-WEBNX; NOC contact noc@webnx.com
  • Address space: announces 159 IPv4 prefixes and 8 IPv6 prefixes, ≈100,096 IPv4 addresses (bgp.he.net); PeeringDB registers 300 IPv4 and 20 IPv6 prefixes. RPKI hygiene is strong: 161 of 167 prefixes carry valid ROAs, zero invalids
  • Capacity: self-reported traffic level 200–300 Gbps, predominantly outbound (PeeringDB) — an outbound-heavy profile typical of content/storage/streaming hosting
  • Public IX ports: Any2West (CoreSite) 100G · Equinix Los Angeles 100G · NYIIX New York 100G · SLIX Salt Lake City 40G
  • Customer-facing ports: 10 Gbps standard on dedicated and GPU servers; 25 Gbps on newer Intel Core Ultra SKUs; 200 Gbps direct-connect between paired DGX Spark units. Bandwidth allowances 50 TB–100 TB outbound per server; colocation sold as 1 Gbps unmetered with 10G available
  • Peering: open policy — no ratio, no minimum traffic, no contract. bgp.he.net observes 78 total BGP peers (76 IPv4, 42 IPv6) across 6 IXs. Transit/upstreams observed: Cogent Communications, Hurricane Electric, NTT America, Zayo Bandwidth, GTT Communications, Arelion (Telia). WebNX's own datacenter page advertises direct uplinks to Level3, NTT, Zayo, Telia and China Unicom
  • Route optimisation: WebNX markets a "BGP route-optimized network"; the adjacent Ogden facility description cites Noction IRP (community-sourced, facility-adjacent)
  • PeeringDB facilities: CoreSite One Wilshire and Equinix LA1 (Los Angeles); DataBank, Flexential Downtown and Level(3) (Salt Lake City); NYI NY1 at 100 William Street (New York)

Sources: PeeringDB net/4729 · bgpview.io/asn/18450

12. Political & public record

Public records only. Every line is tagged. Named principals only. Where nothing exists, that is stated as a finding: a verified null is a result, not a gap.

Headline: the entire documented political footprint of WebNX's named leadership is a single $5.00 contribution made by one person in 2019. Dario Perovich is the only named principal with a federal contribution on record, and the identification is not inferred — the contributor row carries employer "WEBNX, INC", so the filing identifies itself. The other four named principals — Daniel Pautz, Mitchell Pautz, Lynn Ure and Jake Keitz — return verified nulls. WebNX as an entity has no corporate contribution, no corporate PAC, no federal lobbying registration and no Utah lobbyist registration — four separate verified nulls. One loose end is recorded rather than resolved: a same-name donor "URE, LYNN" of Layton UT has 18 ActBlue records, and that identity is not established and is not attributed to the WebNX executive here.

State records — Utah

  • Utah campaign finance — NO RECORDS FOUND for any of the five named principals or for WebNX as an entity, via a mirror of the official data. The primary state portal, disclosures.utah.gov (Utah Lieutenant Governor's Office), is gated behind an image CAPTCHA on every path tried, including the root and /Search/PublicSearch; no bypass was attempted, so the primary portal returned no data. As a substitute, the Accountability Project's mirror of the official dataset "Utah campaign contributions" (publisher: Utah Lt. Governor's Office; 1,382,118 records covering 2000 through 5/10/2023) was searched: pautz → 0; perovich → 0; keitz → 0; "lynn ure" → 0; webnx → 0. A control query (smith → 1,972 results) confirms the search was live and working, so these zeros are genuine negatives, not a broken query. A bare surname query for ure → 56 results, all other Utah Ure donors (Paul Ure of Orem/Woodland, David/Dave Ure of Kamas, Brett Ure of Bountiful, Vicky and Melissa Ure of Kamas, Cheya M. Ure of Heber City, Daylene Ure of Longview TX, Jim Ure of Salt Lake City, Gloria Ure of Lakeland FL, Lincoln Ure) — no Lynn Ure among them. COVERAGE GAP: the mirror ends 5/10/2023, so Utah state contributions from mid-2023 to the present — roughly three years, including the 2024 general election cycle at state level — are not covered by this check (Accountability Project dataset 397 · example query).
  • Utah lobbyist registration — NO REGISTRATION FOUND for any principal or for WebNX. The Utah Lieutenant Governor's lobbyist registry was reachable without a CAPTCHA and its entity-search endpoint was queried directly: webnx → empty result set []; pautz → []; perovich → []; keitz → []. The query ure returned only substring matches inside unrelated names (Lauren Bouton, Lauren Cole, Lauren Handy, Lauren Lambert, Lauren Littlefield, Jacob Magure, Jay Magure, Maureen Mahoney, Lauren Niehaus, Lauren Scholnick) — there is no registered lobbyist or principal named Ure. A control query (web → Webb, LaVarr) confirms the endpoint was returning live data. Neither WebNX nor any named principal is registered as a lobbyist, government officer, or lobbying principal in Utah (lobbyist.utah.gov public search).
  • Municipal and county level — GAP, not searched. Ogden City and Weber County maintain their own local candidate disclosure filings, which are not part of the state dataset. No local-office contribution check was performed for any principal.

Lobbying

GAP — nothing found, and this is a completed search against the authoritative source rather than a portal failure. The Senate/House Lobbying Disclosure Act database (the lda.gov API behind disclosure.senate.gov) returns zero records for WebNX on every axis searched: filings with client_name=webnx → count 0; registrants with registrant_name=webnx → 0; clients with client_name=webnx, "web nx" and "webnx inc" → 0 each; lobbyist-name searches for pautz, perovich and keitz → 0 each. WebNX has therefore never registered as an LDA client, has never retained a registered lobbying firm under its own name, and none of its named principals is a registered federal lobbyist. Disclosed spend: $0 — no LD-1 registration and no LD-2 quarterly activity reports exist, all periods available in the database, searched 2026-08-11 (LDA filings API · registrants · clients · disclosure.senate.gov). Two checks in this area are formally inconclusive rather than negative and are labelled as such: (1) the LDA LD-203 contribution endpoint ignores the payee-name filter and returns the full 673,804-row corpus regardless of query, so it could not be used as a targeted search; (2) the OpenSecrets organisation profile and donor lookup returned a Cloudflare bot interstitial / HTTP 403 on every attempt and no bypass was attempted — given that the LDA database itself shows no registration an OpenSecrets profile would have nothing to report, but treat it as a check not completed, not a confirmed negative (OpenSecrets org lookup).

Searches run

Portals queried that returned matches: FEC OpenFEC Schedule A (contributor-name, contributor-state, contributor-city and contributor-employer axes; the one Perovich row, the 25-row WEBNX employer set, the 18-row Layton "URE, LYNN" set, and the 77-row Ogden URE scan) · FEC committee endpoints (/committee/{id}/, /history/, /filings/ for all eight committee IDs, plus /candidate/P00009183/) · FEC Schedule B (disbursement evidence for C00562983, C00745786, C00633404, C00853481) · houseofnames.com (15 surname pages retrieved) · Accountability Project Utah dataset (control query only) · public web search for company leadership and committee leadership. Portals queried that returned nothing: FEC committee search q=webnx (0 committees) · FEC Schedule A for contributor_name=WEBNX / "WEB NX" (0 rows) and for PAUTZ, MITCHELL/MITCH, KEITZ JAKE/JACOB, and all three surnames restricted to Utah, Ogden and Pleasant View (0 rows each) · Senate/House LDA API (filings, registrants, clients and lobbyists endpoints, 0 each) · Accountability Project Utah dataset (pautz, perovich, keitz, "lynn ure", webnx — 0 each) · lobbyist.utah.gov (webnx, pautz, perovich, keitz — empty result sets). Portals that blocked the check and are recorded as blocked, not null: disclosures.utah.gov (image CAPTCHA on every path, no bypass attempted) · opensecrets.org (Cloudflare interstitial / HTTP 403, no bypass attempted) · forebears.io (HTTP 403 to every access method including a real browser — houseofnames.com was substituted for all onomastic entries, with the URL given in each case) · the LDA /contributions/ LD-203 endpoint (server-side filter broken).

Bottom line on this axis. No legislative or policy position of any kind was found for this company or its principals, and the negative is now a verified one rather than an unsearched assumption. WebNX has no corporate PAC, no federal lobbying registrant, no lobbying spend and no Utah lobbyist registration — and the only confirmed political record attaching to any named principal is a single $5.00 contribution from 2019. On the political and public-policy axis there is nothing to use as a wedge and nothing that creates risk; the material is recorded so that nobody is surprised by it later.

14. Supermicro sales angle

Classification: Whitebox integrator → platform conversion

This is NOT an incumbent-defence account. Supermicro's presence in the WebNX fleet is graded unverified — no chassis, no motherboard, no system-vendor name appears anywhere in WebNX's public material. Opening with "we know you're running Supermicro" will destroy credibility on the first sentence. Turn it into a question instead.

The honest wedge:

WebNX buys at the component and barebones level and integrates in-house — that is confirmed by its own job posting ("assembling & disassembling servers, testing hardware," "repair or replace faulty hardware components," "hundreds of server configurations and parts each year," Indeed). And they have already proven they will buy top-end current-generation AI platforms: dual EPYC 9575F + 4 × RTX PRO 6000 96 GB, publicly listed at $7,999/month (Ogden GPU & AI store). Yet the same catalogue still carries 2017-era V100 and 2020 consumer RTX 3090 nodes, and offers no H100, H200, L40S or GB200 at all.

The angle: GPU-optimised 4U/5U barebones for V100 and RTX 3090 replacement, plus additional dual-Turin 4-GPU and 8-GPU platforms. There is also a natural product fit that Dell and HPE structurally cannot match — WebNX sells AM5 Ryzen 9950X/9950X3D and LGA1851 Core Ultra 9 285K platforms, and Dell and HPE do not build servers around those desktop-class sockets while Supermicro does.

Secondary hooks worth holding in reserve: - The full-rack colocation SKU ($1,199/mo) publishes no amperage allocation, and no kW-per-rack density or PUE exists publicly — density is an unforced discovery topic that leads naturally into AI-platform sizing. - The April 2021 generator fire during the Ogden city-wide outage is a legitimate resiliency conversation and a plausible driver of power-plant and refresh capex. - DGX Spark units are already being paired at 200 Gbps — they are building small multi-node AI clusters and will hit interconnect and density limits.

The one qualifying question for first contact

"For your AM5 and LGA1851 SKUs and your 4-GPU boxes — whose barebones or motherboards are you building on today?"

Ask this and nothing else on the first call. Do not quote. Do not assume an incumbent vendor. One sentence resolves the highest-priority unknown in this account: whether Supermicro is already in WebNX's supply chain at all.

Rule 8 — distributor caution (read before dialling)

WebNX runs a sizeable fleet — self-reported "thousands of high-end servers globally", 150+ racks in Equinix LA3, a 100,000+ sq ft self-operated Ogden facility, ~100,096 IPv4 addresses on AS18450 — yet has never once appeared in the CRM (salesleads Search, Type = All, verified 2026-08-03: no lead, no account, no do-not-call). The only reasonable inference is that their servers are bought through distribution, not direct. Corroborating behaviour: the job posting describes moving "pallets full of servers or other computer parts with a pallet jack" and testing "hundreds of server configurations and parts each year"; LowEndTalk carries threads titled "Regarding WebNX distributors."

Under Rule 8 a distributor may cross territory boundaries, but this requires advance approval and a do-not-call flag. Therefore, before anyone dials: confirm with the channel contact whether WebNX is already an existing account of a distributor. If it is, this shifts from new-business development to a distributor-managed existing account, and the opening, the quoting route and the registration method all change.

Sequence — do not reorder: ① clear the channel/distributor position (Rule 8) → ② register the lead (Ogden UT = West Coast South excl. CA = T1 | T31, Team 1's own territory, direct registration) → ③ make contact, asking only the qualifying question above.

15.3 Open gaps

  1. SYSTEM VENDOR CONFIRMATION — highest priority for this sales team. WebNX names no system vendor, chassis model or motherboard SKU anywhere public. No Supermicro/Dell/HPE evidence exists for WebNX itself. Resolve on the first call with the qualifying question in §14.
  2. Utah Division of Corporations record — entity number, status, formation date and registered agent not retrieved (businessregistration.utah.gov is a JS portal that returned no record via fetch). BBB's "locally incorporated 2018-09-14" must be reconciled against the 1999 founding date.
  3. Revenue — no defensible figure; no estimate asserted. Nothing found supports $100M+.
  4. Power capacity — no MW or kW critical IT load published for Ogden, LA, NY, Dallas or SLC. The full-rack colocation SKU ($1,199/mo) publishes no amperage allocation, and no kW-per-rack density or PUE figure exists publicly. This blocks any AI-density sizing conversation until asked directly.
  5. Ogden facility size conflict — 100,000+ sq ft (own site, datacenters.com) vs nearly 150,000 sq ft (LinkedIn). Unresolved.
  6. Ogden building tenure — "self-operated" / "self-managed" is claimed, but no county property record or lease document was found confirming freehold ownership of 119 N 600 W Bldg 3B versus a long-term lease.
  7. HQ location conflict — BBB, job postings and the CEO's LinkedIn point to Ogden UT; datacenters.com and ZoomInfo say California. Not reconciled.
  8. Exact server and rack counts — only "thousands of high-end servers" (self-reported marketing) and "150+ racks in Equinix LA3" are published. No Ogden rack/cabinet count, no total server inventory, no GPU count.
  9. Named customers: zero. No logo wall, no case studies, no press releases naming clients. Segment inference is derived from SKU mix and ~31,446 domains on 1,707 IPs, not from disclosure.
  10. Named staff beyond CEO and COO: none. No VP Infrastructure, no procurement lead, no hiring manager. Dario Perovich has a name and title from BBB but no verified email and no verified LinkedIn URL. No direct-dial sales contact exists — only the 800 number and support@ / noc@ aliases.
  11. FEC — no federal contribution record exists for founder Daniel Pautz. The single "Daniel J. Pautz" record in FEC data is a Syracuse NY attorney and is a different person — explicitly do not attribute. Dario Perovich was not individually searched.
  12. Dallas (DA1) PoP — sold in the store but absent from the company's own datacenter marketing page; facility name, operator, size and tenure all unknown.
  13. Fetch blocks affecting completeness — WebHostingTalk (HTTP 403) and DataCenterMap (HTTP 429) blocked direct fetch, so the 7-year customer review thread and the DataCenterMap facility record were readable only via search snippets; some corroborating detail in those sources remains unverified at source. datacenters.com (403), ZoomInfo (403) and Crunchbase (403) also blocked. businessregistration.utah.gov and fec.gov's individual-contribution search are JavaScript interfaces that cannot be queried programmatically.
  14. Tooling note for a re-run — the ZoomInfo MCP connector (and carta, figma, atlassian, spglobal, adobe connectors) require OAuth authorisation and could not be used in this non-interactive session. Authorising the ZoomInfo connector via claude.ai connector settings would likely close the revenue-estimate and named-staff gaps on a re-run.

WebNX, Inc. — 銷售情報檔案

編製對象： Supermicro 銷售一組（美國） · 專員 US8664 Tuo Cheng · 日期： 2026-08-04 方法： 以公司歷史、財務與登記資料、美國政治傾向、法案與政策立場，以及該公司與其客戶之關係為研究軸線。本公司為私有公司、無 SEC 申報，證據來自各州商業登記、ARIN／PeeringDB、業者自行公布之伺服器規格與定價、徵才啟事、社群論壇及 FEC 紀錄。每項事實均隨附來源與日期。GAP = 查證資料中未找到，不得憑記憶補寫。 轄區： 猶他州 Ogden — West Coast South excl. CA = T1｜T31。一組可直接註冊。 CRM 狀態： 2026-08-03 於 salesleads Search（Type = All）實查：無 lead、無 account、無 do-not-call 紀錄 — 從未註冊。

1. 結論摘要

WebNX, Inc. 是一家私人持股、由創辦人主導的專用伺服器、主機代管（colocation）與 GPU／AI 託管業者，總部即設於其位於猶他州 Ogden、119 North 600 West, Bldg 3B 的旗艦自營資料中心內，另在洛杉磯（Equinix LA3）、紐約、鹽湖城與達拉斯持有租用據點，營運 AS18450，自述在全球擁有「thousands of high-end servers」，員工規模 11–50 人（LinkedIn 公司頁，自報；BBB 檔案，檔案開立於 2020-06-03）。商業上，這是一個平台轉換（platform conversion）標的，不是既有客戶防守案——WebNX 已確認為元件級自組整合商，在所有公開資料中從未具名任何系統廠，位於銷售一組自有轄區，且在 CRM 中從未出現過任何一筆紀錄。本案最強的單一證據，是 WebNX 自己公開標價的旗艦 AI 機種——雙路 AMD EPYC 9575F（128C/256T）、1.5 TB DDR5、2 × 15 TB Gen5 NVMe、4 張 NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 96 GB，月租 $7,999（Ogden GPU & AI 商店，2026-08-03 讀取）——這證明他們已經在買、也已經在賣當世代 Turin ＋ Blackwell 平台，然而同一份型錄裡仍掛著 2017 年矽晶的 V100 與 2020 年的消費級 RTX 3090，且完全沒有 H100／H200／L40S。唯一可能讓這筆生意破局的因素是通路：這種規模的機隊在 CRM 中卻一筆紀錄都沒有，合理推論是透過經銷通路採購，因此依 Rule 8，必須在撥出第一通電話之前先釐清經銷商歸屬，否則這次接觸會變成通路衝突而不是一筆生意。

3. 原始名單更正表

僅列與本業者相關之列。判定標準：確認＝第一手具名揭露或多方獨立佐證｜部分確認＝核心為真但框架須修正｜反證＝證據與原始名單相反｜無法證實＝公開來源無法建立，不得對客戶陳述。

# 原始名單的說法 判定 證據與正確說法
1 WebNX 使用 Supermicro 和／或 Dell 硬體 無法證實 — 不得對客戶陳述 經 16 次以上檢索並直接抓取 WebNX 的 Ogden custom／GPU-AI／storage／Intel／colocation 商店頁、Utah 機房頁、LinkedIn 與兩則線上 Indeed 職缺，WebNX 從未具名任何系統廠、機殼型號或主機板品牌。其 WHMCS 知識庫為空（「No Articles Found」，knowledgebase），連帶外管理品牌（Supermicro IPMI／Dell iDRAC／HPE iLO）這條慣用辨識線索也斷了。已確立的是： WebNX 是元件級自組整合商——職缺明載「assembling & disassembling servers, testing hardware」「repair or replace faulty hardware components」，以及用棧板車搬運整棧伺服器零件，並自述每年測試「hundreds of server configurations and parts」（Indeed）。全網唯一出現 Dell／SuperMicro／HP 字樣的文字，描述的是 GorillaServers——同一位創辦人（Daniel Pautz）、同一棟 Ogden 廠房、但法人不同——且出自第三方評測而非官方揭露。不得以此推定 WebNX 的機隊。
2 WebNX 提供 GPU 伺服器 確認 — 原始名單中唯一完全成立的硬體相關說法 僅 Ogden 一地即有 14 個公開標價的 GPU SKU，自 $299/月（雙 Xeon Gold 6148 配 GT 1030）到 $7,999/月（雙 AMD EPYC 9575F、1.5 TB DDR5、2 × 15 TB Gen5 NVMe、4 × RTX PRO 6000 96 GB）。洛杉磯與達拉斯另設有獨立的 GPU & AI 商店分類。LinkedIn 專長欄獨立列出「ai server rental」與「hpc rental」。（Ogden GPU & AI 商店，2026-08-03 讀取）
3 WebNX 在猶他州 Ogden 設有資料中心 確認 — 四個獨立來源 WebNX 自家 Utah 機房頁：Ogden 設施逾 100,000 平方英尺，N+1 電力（雙市電饋線、N+1 UPS、多台柴油發電機），直連 Level3、NTT、Zayo、Telia、China Unicom。DataCenterMap 列「WebNX Ogden Data Center, 119 North 600 West, Bldg 3B」。BBB 以同址登錄該公司。WebNX 自家 Indeed 職缺 將機房技師職務置於該址，並寫明 WebNX「operates self-managed data centers across Utah, California, and New York」。2021 年 4 月 Ogden 全市停電期間的發電機起火事件（The Register，2021-04-06）進一步佐證該實體據點存在。（LinkedIn 稱「nearly 150,000 square-foot」屬面積衝突，非位置衝突——見第 15 節。）
4 隱含前提：WebNX 是既有 Supermicro 客戶 反證（就前提而言） 此前提無法成立。在本次研究的業者中，WebNX 是唯一一家伺服器品牌證據為零的業者。以「我們知道你們在用 Supermicro」開場，會在對方第一個問題就破功。正確定位是平台轉換 ＋ 探詢式提問，不是既有客戶防守。
5 第三方名錄（datacenters.com、ZoomInfo）將 WebNX 總部記為加州 反證 — 本檔採 Ogden BBB、兩則 Indeed 職缺與 CEO 本人 LinkedIn 皆將公司置於猶他州 Ogden。CEO、職缺與 BBB 紀錄都在 Ogden。加州之說研判為資料商誤植，或反映其 Equinix LA3 租用據點。來源端仍未和解——已標記，未逕行略去

4. 領導層與所有權

本節證據等級：primary-record（一手紀錄）＝網路號碼登錄機構、法院案卷、聯邦競選財務申報、主管機關檔案，或公司自家已發布頁面｜corroborated（多方佐證）＝兩個以上獨立次級來源互相印證｜single-source（單一來源）＝僅一個次級來源，無其他佐證｜GAP＝已具名搜尋但查無。

進入表格前有兩項前提。第一，2026 年現行的 webnx.com 完全沒有具名任何人：該站為前端渲染的 React SPA，/about、/contact、/company/the-team/ 三個路徑回傳的是同一份 2,166 bytes 的空殼，其 schema.org Organization 區塊的創辦人欄位是字面上的佔位字串 "WebNX Founder"。因此以下每一個姓名都來自登錄機構、政府申報、公司自家舊版團隊頁的存檔，或次級來源，沒有一個來自現行官網。第二，登錄機構幹部與網路聯絡人以獨立列呈現並明確標示：ARIN OrgAdmin 或 IRR admin-c 是有名有姓的真人，且是公開合法的接觸管道；對未上市公司而言，這往往是唯一能取得的幹部等級姓名。
